<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which EC number best matches the protein’s principal catalytic activity: hydrolysis of N-(long-chain fatty acyl)ethanolamines to fatty acids and ethanolamine?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> EC 3.5.1.4<br><br><strong>B.</strong> EC 3.5.1.49<br><br><strong>C.</strong> EC 3.5.1.1<br><br><strong>D.</strong> EC 3.5.1.60<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: The question asks about the hydrolysis of N-(long-chain fatty acyl)ethanolamines to fatty acids and ethanolamine. Let me evaluate each option:<br><br>- EC 3.5.1.4 is amidase (general acylase), which hydrolyzes linear amides broadly.<br>- EC 3.5.1.49 is N-acyl-aliphatic-L-amino acid amidohydrolase, which hydrolyzes N-acyl amino acids.<br>- EC 3.5.1.1 is amidinase, which hydrolyzes amidines.<br>- EC 3.5.1.60 is fatty acid amide hydrolase (FAAH), which specifically catalyzes the hydrolysis of N-(long-chain fatty acyl)ethanolamines to fatty acids and ethanolamine.<br><br>The protein sequence shows key features of FAAH:<br>- An N-terminal transmembrane domain (hydrophobic region: MGTPAIRAACHGAHLALALLLLLSLSD)<br>- The amidase signature family motif, including the characteristic &quot;TATTF&quot; region and the &quot;SGDERDKGWWWEN&quot; region containing the catalytic Ser-Ser-Lys triad<br>- The &quot;GDRxW&quot; motif near the active site<br><br>The reaction described in the question — hydrolysis of N-(long-chain fatty acyl)ethanolamines to fatty acids and ethanolamine — is the defining reaction of EC 3.5.1.60 (fatty acid amide hydrolase/FAAH).<br><br>2. answer: D</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For the protein represented by the sequence below, which EC number best fits its principal catalytic function? In enzyme catalysis:

- Asp acts as the charge relay (or orienting residue) that stabilizes the positive charge that develops on His through hydrogen bonding

- His = proton acceptor
- Asp = charge relay

<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> What is the best-supported subcellular location for this protein?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Cytosolic<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Nuclear<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Mitochondrial<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Secreted<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>**Analysis of the N-terminus:**<br>The protein begins with &quot;MNNSIILIFVAILIIF...&quot; which contains a distinctive hydrophobic stretch (IILIFVAILIIF) from approximately position 5-17. Cytosolic:** Cytosolic proteins are synthesized on free ribosomes and lack signal peptides. The presence of a clear N-terminal signal peptide rules out a cytosolic location.<br><br>- **B. Nuclear:** Nuclear proteins have nuclear localization signals (NLS) — clusters of basic residues (K, R). The N-terminal hydrophobic stretch is not consistent with an NLS.<br><br>- **C. Mitochondrial:** Mitochondrial targeting sequences are typically amphipathic helices rich in basic (Arg, Lys) and hydroxylated (Ser, Thr) residues, not highly hydrophobic stretches like the one seen here.<br><br>- **D. Additionally, the protein contains multiple N-glycosylation sites (N-X-S/T motifs like NVT, NMT, NQT), which are characteristic of secreted proteins that pass through the ER/Golgi. The overall composition and the clear signal peptide strongly support secretion.<br><br>**Conclusion:** The presence of a canonical N-terminal signal peptide combined with multiple glycosylation sites strongly indicates this protein is secreted.<br><br>2. answer: D</td>
